## Case 100
Edit: sentence inserted: "The patient's father has dialysis-dependent kidney disease."

Item: Renal disease. Stated: this item scores 0 point(s) for the edited note.

**Score definition**

```
The criteria for the HAS-BLED score are listed below below:

1. Hypertension (Uncontrolled, >160 mmHg systolic): No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point
2. Renal disease (Dialysis, transplant, Cr >2.26 mg/dL or >200 µmol/L): No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point
3. Liver disease (Cirrhosis or bilirubin >2x normal with AST/ALT/AP >3x normal): No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point
4. Stroke history: No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point
5. Prior major bleeding or predisposition to bleeding: No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point
6. Labile INR (Unstable/high INRs, time in therapeutic range <60%): No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point
7. Age >65: No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point
8. Medication usage predisposing to bleeding (Aspirin, clopidogrel, NSAIDs): No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point
9. Alcohol use (≥8 drinks/week): No = 0 points, Yes = +1 point

The total HAS-BLED score is calculated by summing the points for each criterion.
```

**Original note**

A 57-year-old woman arrived to the hospital-based anticoagulation management clinic for a scheduled evaluation of her long-term vitamin K antagonist therapy, which had been instituted sixteen months earlier after electrocardiographic confirmation of paroxysmal atrial fibrillation discovered during an emergency department visit for palpitations and mild light-headedness. Since initiation of warfarin she has attended weekly or bi-weekly laboratory checks, yet despite diligent follow-up the anticoagulation service has documented marked swings in her international normalized ratio; printouts reviewed today demonstrate values dipping to 1.6 and soaring to 4.3 on several occasions over the past quarter, requiring alternating dose escalations, temporary holds, and two instances of oral vitamin K rescue, and her calculated time in therapeutic range is well below the recommended threshold. She has never required hospitalization, reversal with plasma, or transfusion, and she denies epistaxis, gingival oozing, gross hematuria, hematemesis, melena, hematochezia, or abnormal bruising. Her past medical history is noteworthy for a non-ST-elevation myocardial infarction four years ago treated with a drug-eluting stent to the right coronary artery; consequently she has remained on low-dose aspirin 81 mg daily as secondary prevention. In addition, she manages chronic mechanical low-back pain with over-the-counter ibuprofen 400 mg up to three times per day during flares, most recently in the week prior to clinic because she helped her daughter move apartments. She does not carry a diagnosis of high blood pressure; serial outpatient readings furnished by her primary care physician and today’s measurement of 118/70 mm Hg confirm normotension, and she takes no antihypertensive agents. There is no personal history of transient ischemic attack, ischemic or hemorrhagic stroke, intracranial aneurysm, carotid artery disease, peripheral embolic events, peptic ulcer disease, inflammatory bowel disease, malignancy, inherited bleeding diathesis, or chronic viral hepatitis. She has never noticed jaundice, pruritus, abdominal distention, confusion, or easy fatigability suggestive of hepatocellular dysfunction; a comprehensive metabolic panel obtained one month ago for routine surveillance showed alanine aminotransferase 22 U/L, aspartate aminotransferase 25 U/L, alkaline phosphatase 72 U/L, total bilirubin 0.7 mg/dL, and albumin 4.3 g/dL, values consistent with preserved synthetic capacity. Her other long-standing conditions include well‐controlled hyperlipidemia on rosuvastatin 20 mg nightly and seasonal allergic rhinitis for which she uses intranasal corticosteroids as needed. She does not smoke and has never used recreational drugs. Regarding alcohol, she candidly reports enjoying two glasses of red wine with dinner on most weeknights and often sharing a couple of craft beers with friends on Saturdays, yielding an average of 11 alcoholic drinks per week; she denies binge episodes, hard liquor intake, or morning consumption. Family history is pertinent for premature coronary disease in her father but negative for hemorrhagic stroke, aneurysm, or clotting disorders. Review of systems today is negative for chest pain, new dyspnea, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, peripheral edema, abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, change in bowel habits, focal weakness, vision changes, or sudden severe headache. She works as a middle school science teacher, walks two miles most mornings, and maintains independence in all activities of daily living. On examination she appears well nourished and in no distress, with body mass index 24 kg/m², temperature 36.8 °C, irregularly irregular pulse 86 beats/min, respiratory rate 14/min, and oxygen saturation 98 % on ambient air. Conjunctiva are pink, sclerae anicteric, oropharyngeal mucosa moist without petechiae, and neck supple without jugular venous distention. Cardiac auscultation reveals the expected variability in S1 intensity without murmurs, rubs, or gallops. Lungs are clear to auscultation throughout. The abdomen is soft, nondistended, and non-tender with no palpable hepatosplenomegaly, shifting dullness, or caput medusae. Skin is warm without ecchymoses, purpura, spider angiomas, or palmar erythema; distal pulses are intact and there is no pedal edema. Neurologic screening shows normal mentation, intact cranial nerves, full motor strength, and normal coordination. Point-of-care INR obtained in clinic is 3.9, once again above her target range of 2.0–3.0. A complete blood count drawn simultaneously demonstrates hemoglobin 13.1 g/dL, hematocrit 39 %, and platelet count 245 × 10⁹/L, with normal indices. Electrocardiogram confirms atrial fibrillation with controlled ventricular response at 80 beats/min and no acute ischemic changes. She expresses concern about the difficulty maintaining stable anticoagulation and the cumulative effect of aspirin and intermittent ibuprofen on her risk of bleeding, yet she wishes to continue stroke prophylaxis given her understanding of the arrhythmia’s thromboembolic potential. The encounter focuses on education regarding limiting nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug use, moderating alcohol intake, coordinating with her cardiologist to determine if she might transition to a direct oral anticoagulant once contraindications are excluded, and scheduling closer follow-up with the anticoagulation team to address dietary fluctuations that may be contributing to her variable INR.

She has never noticed jaundice, pruritus, abdominal distention, confusion, or easy fatigability suggestive of hepatocellular dysfunction; a comprehensive metabolic panel obtained one month ago for routine surveillance showed alanine aminotransferase 22 U/L, aspartate aminotransferase 25 U/L, alkaline phosphatase 72 U/L, total bilirubin 0.7 mg/dL, and albumin 4.3 g/dL, values consistent with preserved synthetic capacity. Her other long-standing conditions include well‐controlled hyperlipidemia on rosuvastatin 20 mg nightly and seasonal allergic rhinitis for which she uses intranasal corticosteroids as needed. She does not smoke and has never used recreational drugs. Regarding alcohol, she candidly reports enjoying two glasses of red wine with dinner on most weeknights and often sharing a couple of craft beers with friends on Saturdays, yielding an average of 11 alcoholic drinks per week; she denies binge episodes, hard liquor intake, or morning consumption. Family history is pertinent for premature coronary disease in her father but negative for hemorrhagic stroke, aneurysm, or clotting disorders. Review of systems today is negative for chest pain, new dyspnea, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, peripheral edema, abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, change in bowel habits, focal weakness, vision changes, or sudden severe headache. She works as a middle school science teacher, walks two miles most mornings, and maintains independence in all activities of daily living. On examination she appears well nourished and in no distress, with body mass index 24 kg/m², temperature 36.8 °C, irregularly irregular pulse 86 beats/min, respiratory rate 14/min, and oxygen saturation 98 % on ambient air. Conjunctiva are pink, sclerae anicteric, oropharyngeal mucosa moist without petechiae, and neck supple without jugular venous distention. Cardiac auscultation reveals the expected variability in S1 intensity without murmurs, rubs, or gallops. Lungs are clear to auscultation throughout. The abdomen is soft, nondistended, and non-tender with no palpable hepatosplenomegaly, shifting dullness, or caput medusae. Skin is warm without ecchymoses, purpura, spider angiomas, or palmar erythema; distal pulses are intact and there is no pedal edema. Neurologic screening shows normal mentation, intact cranial nerves, full motor strength, and normal coordination. Point-of-care INR obtained in clinic is 3.9, once again above her target range of 2.0–3.0. A complete blood count drawn simultaneously demonstrates hemoglobin 13.1 g/dL, hematocrit 39 %, and platelet count 245 × 10⁹/L, with normal indices. Electrocardiogram confirms atrial fibrillation with controlled ventricular response at 80 beats/min and no acute ischemic changes. She expresses concern about the difficulty maintaining stable anticoagulation and the cumulative effect of aspirin and intermittent ibuprofen on her risk of bleeding, yet she wishes to continue stroke prophylaxis given her understanding of the arrhythmia’s thromboembolic potential. The encounter focuses on education regarding limiting nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug use, moderating alcohol intake, coordinating with her cardiologist to determine if she might transition to a direct oral anticoagulant once contraindications are excluded, and scheduling closer follow-up with the anticoagulation team to address dietary fluctuations that may be contributing to her variable INR.
